#### `counts-NUM-1` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 11; 4.8 Floating text: explanations on the canvas (timing paragraph); category numeric-accuracy
- **Issue:** MISMATCH: 'During autoplay, its duration is limited by the step interval and configured delay; notes are suppressed for intervals below 200 milliseconds' misstates the code. During autoplay a note lasts min(1,600 ms, 0.9 x step interval - delay), and it is dropped when that result is under 200 ms. Step notes are always created with delay 0, so they disappear when the step interval is below about 222 ms, not 200 ms, and no configured delay applies to them. The evidence pack states this correctly (doc 02 line 122); the report's paraphrase lost the 0.9 factor. The 1,600 ms life when stepping by hand is a MATCH.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** visualgo-plus-design@662e7b3: prototypes/src/shared/visualization/floatingTextTiming.ts:11 (FLOATING_NOTE_MS = 1600), :13 (FLOATING_TEXT_MIN_MS = 200), :27-29 (cutToStep: Math.min(lifeMs, effectiveIntervalMs * 0.9 - delayMs); undefined when < 200). prototypes/src/shared/visualization/stepNotes.ts:45 (floatingNoteDurationMs(arrival), called with no delay) and :48 (delayMs: 0). floatingTextTiming.test.ts:22 (1000 ms interval -> 900 ms), :29 (223 ms -> 201 ms, shown), :30 (200 ms -> none). docsforfyp/02_contribution_evidence.md:122.
- **Proposed fix:** Replace the two sentences with: 'When the learner steps forward by hand, a note lasts 1,600 milliseconds. During autoplay it is cut to 90% of the step interval and left out when less than 200 milliseconds would remain (steps shorter than about 222 milliseconds).' Delete 'and configured delay'.
- **Verifier:** I reproduced this in the code. floatingTextTiming.ts:27-29 sets a note's autoplay life to min(1600, 0.9 x effectiveIntervalMs - delay) and drops the note when that is under 200 ms. stepNotes.ts:45 and :48 always pass a delay of 0, so text notes disappear for step intervals below about 222 ms, not 200 ms. The test at floatingTextTiming.test.ts:29 shows a 223 ms interval giving a 201 ms note, and :30 shows 200 ms giving none. The band is reachable: the graph's minimum interval is 50 ms (graph/visualization/sessionReducer.ts:3). Only the segment-tree number popups pass a delay (tree/algorithms/segment/valuePopups.ts:91). Doc 02:122 states the rule correctly; the 1,600 ms life when stepping by hand matches.

#### `counts-NUM-2` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 9; 4.6 Graph extensions and algorithm validation (randomized cases); category numeric-accuracy
- **Issue:** The total of 700 is correct, but the sentence follows 'Cut vertices and bridges are checked by removing a vertex or edge and recomputing connected components', so it reads as 700 comparisons against that oracle. Only seeds 1-400 are compared with the brute force. Seeds 401-700 (300 graphs) check the root rule (a DFS root is a cut vertex iff it has two or more DFS children) against the same run's own DFS tree, which is not an independent oracle. The numbers are also hard to trace. The nearest citation, [7] (doc 04 section 2.4), records only 'seeds 1-400' and gives neither 140 nor 20. The 400 + 300, 140 and 20 figures appear in doc 02 section 2.2, which is part of [8]. The 140 (Bellman-Ford, 2 x 70 fixtures, every source) and 20 (Prim, every start vertex) are MATCH.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** prototypes/src/graph/algorithms/cutVertexBridge/cutVertexBridgeProperty.test.ts:81-86 (seeds 1-400 compared with bruteForce) and :123-124 (seeds 401-700, root rule, no bruteForce call). bellmanFord/createBellmanFordSequence.test.ts:241-260 (directed x 70 fixtures, every source, Floyd-Warshall oracle, assert cases >= 140). prim/primEngine.test.ts:264 (20 documents) and :278-288 (every start, exhaustive forest search). docsforfyp/04_figures_and_validation.md:176-178. docsforfyp/02_contribution_evidence.md:98-100.
- **Proposed fix:** Write: 'The recorded randomized cases include 700 random multigraphs for cut vertices and bridges (400 compared with the removal oracle and 300 checking the DFS-root rule), 140 signed graphs for Bellman-Ford from every source, and 20 small graphs for Prim from every start vertex [8].' Keep [7] on the topological/bipartite sentence.
- **Verifier:** I reproduced the split. cutVertexBridgeProperty.test.ts:86 compares seeds 1-400 with bruteForce. Lines :123-124 check seeds 401-700 only against the root rule, using the same run's own parentByNodeId, which is not an independent oracle. '700 graph inputs' is literally true, but it follows the sentence about the removal oracle, so it reads as 700 oracle comparisons. The 700/140/20 sentence has no citation of its own. The nearest one, [7], points to doc 04:176-178, which says 'seeds 1-400' and gives no 140 or 20. The figures 400 + 300, 140 and 20 are in doc 02 section 2.2 (part of [8]). The other two counts match: 140 = directed and undirected x 70 fixtures (createBellmanFordSequence.test.ts:241-260), and 20 Prim documents (primEngine.test.ts:264, :278).

#### `counts-NUM-3` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 7; 4.4 V10: a shared panel-group layout (minimums paragraph); category numeric-accuracy
- **Issue:** The report presents 'the drawing has a target floor of 360 by 320 pixels' as the floor of the shell shared by all three modules. In fact 360 x 320 is the graph's floor and the shell's default only. This is a MISMATCH for tree and sorting. Tree workspaces pass 320 x 200 (LCA, BST, AVL, Trie) or 320 x 240 (Heap and both Segment Trees). Sorting passes 320 x 200 in Study and a taller floor that depends on the pane count in Compare. The 240 px width / 120 px height group minimums are a MATCH (for expanded groups; a collapsed group is its 31 px strip). The evidence pack makes the same simplification (note 03:202, note 09:167, doc 04 D2), although V10 choice 10 records the per-module floors.

In Compare, src/sorting/app/drawingFloor.ts:100-110 with VizPage.tsx:293 gives 44 + rows x 234 (+7 px gap), i.e. 278 px for two panes and 519 px for four (my arithmetic). #### `architecture-ARCH-4` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 6 (also 13); 4.3 para 2; 6.2; category factual-precision
- **Issue:** The sorting limitation sentence is wrong in two ways. (1) History is never trimmed, but it is not unbounded: every input is capped at 240 values, and a code comment records the worst-case size. (2) 'Distant seeks can require substantial replay' gets the cost model wrong. A backward seek always replays from a fresh state up to the target, so its cost grows with the target's position, not with how far the seek jumps. In addition, at the app's input sizes the 60,000-operation priming usually holds the whole comparison, so the lazy path only matters for the largest combinations.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** docsforfyp report/main.tex:195, and :312 ('Sorting retains unbounded history'). visualgo-plus-design prototypes/src/sorting/app/useSortingExperiment.ts:25 sets MAX_CUSTOM_VALUES = 240, enforced on the resolved input at :90-91. Bao's comment above EAGER_OPERATION_CAP (src/sorting/comparison/operationBuffer.ts:232-246, from 5ccef45) records counts: shipped 4-pane default at n=48 = 3,356 operations; n=160 = 25,015; n=240 = 53,034; worst 4-pane choice at n=240 = 159,298; 105-180 bytes per operation. Priming runs to 60,000 summed across panes (:292-327). For a backward seek, seekComparison calls replayOperations(input, run.history.slice(0, consumed)) (src/sorting/comparison/comparisonPlayback.ts:346-352). replayOperations is applySortOperations(createPlaybackState(input), operations) (src/sorting/engine/operations.ts:446-451). A one-step Previous instead reverts a single operation (retreatComparison, comparisonPlayback.ts:257-300).
- **Proposed fix:** In main.tex:195, replace the second sentence with: 'However, retained history is never trimmed (only the 240-value input cap bounds it), and a backward seek replays the retained operations from the start up to the target, so its cost grows with how late the target lies rather than with the distance jumped.' main.tex:312 can keep 'unbounded' or read 'never trims its history'.
- **Verifier:** Seek cost: reproduced. A backward seekComparison replays from a fresh state via replayOperations(input, run.history.slice(0, consumed)) (comparisonPlayback.ts:346-352; engine/operations.ts:446-451). Its cost therefore grows with the target's position, not with how far the seek jumps; a forward seek is a batch advance and Previous reverts one operation (:257-300). 'Unbounded': overstated as an error. The project's own records use that word for never-trimmed history (prototypes/docs/sorting/IMPROVEMENT_BACKLOG.md:61-63; sorting-performance-milestone.md:139), and the 240-value cap (useSortingExperiment.ts:25, 90-91) bounds it in practice. Stating that bound adds precision; it does not correct an error. Drop the colleague's 'priming usually holds the whole comparison' clause: whether it holds depends on the algorithm mix (worst four-pane case 159,298 operations against a 60,000 cap), so it adds a claim nobody has tested.

#### `algorithms-ALGFIG-6` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 9; 4.6 Graph extensions and algorithm validation, paragraph 1; category precision
- **Issue:** "The traversal interface also supports an End node where applicable" does not say which algorithms have it. Only BFS, Dijkstra and Bellman–Ford take an End node. It is a shortest-path query whose path is drawn on the run's last frame (DD-45). The control is visible as "END NODE All vertices" in Figures 3, 4 and 8 (F2/F3/F4 PNGs).
- **Proposed fix:** 'BFS, Dijkstra and Bellman–Ford also accept an optional End node; their last frame then draws the shortest path to it [8].' Optionally add: 'visualgo.net's SCC and 2-SAT, struck through in the bar in Figures 3–5, are not built yet.'
- **Verifier:** Checked. Only 'bfs' / 'dijkstra' / 'bellman-ford' take an End node (shortestPathQuery.ts:11, 53-60), and the run form renders it only for those three (AlgorithmRunForm.tsx:138-147). The path is drawn on the last frame only (isShortestPathFrame). F8 shows no End node for CVB. 'where applicable' is true but tells the reader nothing, and 'traversal interface' is wrong for Dijkstra and Bellman-Ford, which run on the SSSP page. The colleague's '(DD-45)' does not fit, because the report cites no DD ids anywhere.

#### `floating-FT-3` (Minor, finder said Important; CONFIRMED)

Project docs at 662e7b3 say 'step note' 31 times and 'floating text' 10 times. prototypes/AGENTS.md:15: 'the floating text a step shows as it arrives, with the step notes' layer'. - In the report, 'floating text' appears on p1, p4, p11 (heading and para 1), p13 (twice) and p16; 'step notes' appears on p4 (§3.1, Table 1, §3.2), p5 (Fig. 1), p11 (body) and p13 (§6.2).
- **Proposed fix:** Say once that the two terms mean the same thing; no global rename. In §4.8 para 1: '... I introduced floating text, implemented as step notes: short explanations ...'. Optionally add that it is the text form of the floating value popups (+7) added to the Segment Trees on 13 October (TP-20). In §3.1 para 4: '... added step notes (the floating text of Section 4.8) ...'. Use one term in both §6.2 sentences.
- **Verifier:** The core holds. 'Floating text' (p1, p4 §3.1, p11, p13 §6.1 and §6.2, p16) and 'step notes' (p4 §3.1, Table 1 and §3.2; p5 Fig. 1; p13 §6.2) are never said to be the same thing. §3.1 even reads them as two items: the trial led to 'floating text', and after 18 Oct the work 'added step notes'. #### `floating-FT-5` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 11; 4.8 para 1 and placement paragraph; category technical-accuracy
- **Issue:** 'Short explanations beside the relevant node or edge' and 'the anchor follows the relevant node or edge' overstate what the code does. - A note anchors only to a node, or to the top of the canvas when it is about the whole drawing. - A Dijkstra note sits over the target vertex of the relaxed edge; edge-weight labels act only as obstacles. - The default position is centred above the node; beside and below are fallbacks. - 'Outside the graph transform' is too narrow, because the same layer also serves the tree canvas.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** - prototypes/src/shared/visualization/stepNotes.ts:10-19: StepNotes holds nodeNotes (each with a nodeId) and canvasNotes. - shared/components/canvas/StepNoteLayer.tsx:67-77. - shared/visualization/stepNotePlacement.ts:182-211: canvas notes go at the top centre or a top corner; node notes go above the node, else right, left or below. - graph/algorithms/dijkstra/dijkstraStepNotes.ts:28-31 calls write.onNode(targetNodeId, ...). DD-61, docs/graph/DESIGN_DECISIONS.md:2691: 'The anchor is relaxation.targetNodeId'. - graph/components/graph/stepNoteAnchor.ts:181-211 returns the weight labels as obstacles. - tree/components/tree/StepNoteLayer.tsx:42-46: the tree mounts the same shared layer. - The F4 image shows the note over E, not on the edge B→E.
- **Proposed fix:** §4.8 para 1: '... short explanations over the relevant node (for Dijkstra, the vertex the relaxed edge reaches), or at the top of the canvas'. Placement paragraph: '... constant screen size while each note follows its node'.
- **Verifier:** I reproduced this. Notes are either nodeNotes (each with a nodeId) or canvasNotes (stepNotes.ts:10-19; writer at :30-34; StepNoteLayer.tsx:67-77). Dijkstra calls onNode(targetNodeId) (dijkstraStepNotes.ts:31). Weight labels only act as obstacles (stepNoteAnchor.ts:188-211). F4 shows the note over E. Only 'or edge' is wrong; 'beside' instead of 'above' and 'graph transform' are loose wording, not errors.

#### `floating-FT-7` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Issue:** The section's motivating example is a rejected relaxation, but Figure 8 shows an accepted update, and the text never says that rejected relaxations get notes. Equal candidates are rejected too, so 'the existing distance is already smaller' is narrower than the actual rule.

- **Proposed fix:** Add one sentence after Figure 8: 'Each relaxation writes one note over its target: green when the distance changes, as here, and red when it is kept, e.g. "3 + 5 > 4 · keep" or "already settled".' Dropping the duplicate Status sentence (FT-4) keeps the length the same. Recapturing Figure 8 at a keep frame (DD-61 table, graph DESIGN_DECISIONS.md:2657) is optional.
- **Verifier:** I reproduced this. dijkstraStepNotes.ts:33-43 writes green notes ('0 + 4 = 4', '... < ... · update') and red ones ('... = ... · keep', '... > ... · keep', 'already settled'). F4 shows only the green update over E, on a step where the drawing does change (badge 8→7, edge turns green). The section's motivating case, a step that leaves the drawing almost unchanged, is never shown, and the red/green coding is never named. The suggested 'no larger' rewording is unnecessary: 'may reject ... because the existing distance is already smaller' is a correct example.

#### `literature-LIT-3` (Minor, finder said Important; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 3 (also 11 and 14); 2.1 (also 4.8 on p11 and 6.3/Table 4 on pp13-14); category critical-use
- **Issue:** Hundhausen et al. [3] is cited accurately, but only to separate 'does the software behave correctly' from 'do students learn'. The report never applies the paper's central result: what learners do matters more than what they see. That result bears directly on the main post-trial feature (floating text, which learners only view), on input editing (a what-if activity) and on the planned follow-up tasks.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** [3] abstract (faculty.cc.gatech.edu/~stasko/papers/jvlc02.pdf, text extracted): 'a systematic meta-study of 24 experimental studies ... Our most significant finding is that how students use AV technology has a greater impact on effectiveness than what AV technology shows them.' The p3 paraphrase is faithful. Section 6.3 of the paper (p. 284): 'studies in which students merely viewed visualizations did not demonstrate significant learning advantages over students who used conventional learning materials'. The successful uses it names engage students in 'what-if analyses', 'prediction exercises' and 'programming exercises'. Section 6.2: 'procedural knowledge may serve as a more sensitive measure ... than conceptual knowledge'. Pre/post improvement is more sensitive, but 'the pre-test may give participants knowledge of the target algorithm'. In the report, p3 (main.tex:96) uses [3] only for the two-questions split. Section 4.8 (p11) presents floating text as reducing attention switching, and Table 4 (p14) plans 'Correct prediction of the next swap' and explanation tasks; neither refers back to [3].
- **Proposed fix:** Add one sentence to 4.8: 'Because viewing alone has rarely improved learning [3], the notes will be evaluated through active tasks (Table 4).' Cite [3] in the Table 4 caption or in 6.3. Optionally write '24 experimental studies' on p3. Use Naps et al.'s engagement-level names only after reading the original.
- **Verifier:** The quotes match jvlc02.txt (abstract; §6.2; §6.3 on p. 284). But 'never applies' overstates the problem. p3 does use [3]'s central finding to separate correctness from learning, and Table 4 already plans prediction ('Correct prediction of the next swap') and explanation tasks. What is missing is the link to floating text. Floating text is a viewing aid, and [3] reports that 'studies in which students merely viewed visualizations did not demonstrate significant learning advantages'. The paper says '24 experimental studies', not 'controlled experiments'. Severity is downgraded from Important because this overlaps LIT-2 and can be fixed inside that rewrite.

#### `literature-LIT-4` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 3; 2.1; References [2]; category citation-accuracy
- **Issue:** The report says Halim et al. 2012 'present VisuAlgo', but that paper never uses the name. The source that does name VisuAlgo (Halim 2015) also documents VisuAlgo's Online Quiz. That quiz is a closer comparison than OpenDSA for the report's 'assessed learning sequence' point, and VisuAlgo+ has deliberately not built it.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** Halim et al. 2012 PDF (https://ioi.te.lv/oi/pdf/INFOL099.pdf, HTTP 200, text extracted): no occurrence of 'VisuAlgo' (case-insensitive); the tool is at 'http://www.comp.nus.edu.sg/stevenha/visualization'. Its abstract supports both properties the report attributes to it ('users ... can enter their own input data'; 'a consistent user interface across different algorithm visualizations'). It also lists '(4) ... accessible on modern portable PCs including tablets and smartphones'. Halim 2015, Olympiads in Informatics 9:243-245, doi:10.15388/ioi.2015.20 (Crossref; PDF ioinformatics.org/journal/v9_2015_243_245.pdf, HTTP 200), says 'VisuAlgo ... is the continuation of the work that was presented in IOI conference 3 years ago (Halim et al. 2012)' and 'we have added an important learning component: An Online Quiz tool ... graded instantly'. Report: main.tex:94 'Halim et al. [2] present VisuAlgo'; main.tex:98 'It does not yet provide an equivalent assessed learning sequence'. App at 662e7b3: prototypes/src/site/HomePage.tsx:203-204, '/* Training is not built. A badge on every card would say it is. */ quizAvailable={false}'.
- **Proposed fix:** On p3, write: 'Halim et al. [2] present the unified, interactive web-based collection that later became VisuAlgo [Halim 2015]', and add the 2015 entry to the references. In the OpenDSA paragraph, add: 'VisuAlgo itself offers an instantly graded Online Quiz (Training) [Halim 2015]; VisuAlgo+ has not rebuilt it.' Also list Training among the p2 scope exclusions.
- **Verifier:** I reproduced this: halim2012.txt has 0 hits for 'visualgo', and the tool's URL is comp.nus.edu.sg/stevenha/visualization. Halim 2015 (Crossref doi:10.15388/ioi.2015.20) calls VisuAlgo 'the continuation' of that work and adds 'An Online Quiz tool'. visualgo.net/en itself describes the 2012 paper as 'our 2012 paper about this system (it was not yet called VisuAlgo back in 2012)'. It says the 2015 update is there 'to link VisuAlgo name with the previous project', and that 'VisuAlgo has two main components: The 24 visualization pages and their associated Online Quiz component'. At 662e7b3, HomePage.tsx:203-204 reads 'Training is not built'. The report never mentions the quiz or Training.

The PDF's link annotation is the broken https://opendsa.org URL. The colleague's evidence is wrong on one point: 'proficiency' does appear on the page, three times. #### `literature-LIT-7` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 3; 2.2 Reusable platforms and implementation choices; category critical-use
- **Issue:** Shaffer et al. [5] is used only for a generic point ('helps motivate attention to maintainable implementations'). The report leaves out the paper's specific findings that would position VisuAlgo+'s topic choices: sorting is over-represented, advanced topics are poorly covered, and AVs get lost over time.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** Shaffer et al. 2010, author copy (people.cs.vt.edu/~shaffer/Papers/ShafferTOCE11, text extracted), pp. 9:14-9:15: 'many of these sorting AVs ... merely show bars being swapped. In contrast, most specialized and advanced topics are poorly covered.' Abstract: 'many AVs are of low quality, and coverage is skewed toward a few easier topics ... There are no effective repositories ... which puts many AVs at risk for being lost'. p. 9:13: virtually all AVs since the mid-1990s 'were implemented in Java'. Report: main.tex:101 (p3). VisuAlgo+ includes Lazy Segment Tree, LCA (binary lifting, prototypes/src/tree/algorithms/lca/binaryLifting/), cut vertices and bridges, and Edmonds-Karp (pp. 9-10), plus a synchronized sorting Compare mode (p10).
- **Proposed fix:** Replace 'Their historical survey helps motivate...' with: 'Shaffer et al. found that most AVs were Java applets or Web Start programs, that no effective repositories existed, and that many AVs risked being lost, with coverage skewed to easy topics [5]. Rebuilding VisuAlgo on a maintained web stack addresses the first risk while keeping its advanced topics (e.g. lazy segment tree, LCA by binary lifting, cut vertices and bridges).' Keep the existing disclaimer.
- **Verifier:** The quotes match shaffer.txt. Abstract: 'coverage is skewed toward a few easier topics' and 'no effective repositories ... at risk for being lost'. p. 9:13: 'implemented in Java'. p. 9:14: 'merely show bars being swapped ... advanced topics are poorly covered'. The p3 sentence is vague. Two corrections to the fix. The most relevant link is obsolescence and loss, which motivates objective 1. Advanced-topic coverage is VisuAlgo's achievement that the rebuild keeps, and quoting 'bars being swapped' invites comparison with the sorting module's own bars.

#### `literature-added-1` (Minor; ADDED BY VERIFIER)

- **Where:** page 3; 2.1 (user-defined inputs); also 4.2 on p6 (backward stepping); category critical-use
- **Issue:** Section 2 omits the experimental AV-features study that tests properties the report says a rebuild should preserve. Saraiya et al. found that pace control mattered most, and that a good example data set beat student-built data. A pseudocode display and the ability to back up showed no significant effect. The report presents user-defined inputs, pseudocode and backward stepping as core properties without engaging this evidence. It also does not use the study's support for curated examples, which the app keeps.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** Crossref doi:10.1145/971300.971432: Saraiya, Shaffer, McCrickard, North, 'Effective features of algorithm visualizations', SIGCSE '04, 382-386. Abstract via OpenAlex: 'The single most important feature studied is the ability to directly control the pace'; 'Providing a good example ... proved significantly more effective than letting students construct their own data sets'; 'a pseudocode display, a series of questions ..., or the ability to back up within the visualization did not show a significant effect on learning'. Report main.tex:94: 'Support for user-defined inputs ... is particularly relevant'. p6: 'makes backward stepping and seeking straightforward'. The app copies VisuAlgo's example graphs (visualgoExamples.ts:1-7 at 662e7b3; doc 05:51, '37 example graphs', DD-48). I checked the metadata and abstract only; I did not read the full text.
- **Proposed fix:** After reading the paper, add 1-2 sentences to 2.1, for example: 'In controlled experiments, pace control mattered most and a good example beat student-constructed data, while pseudocode display and backing up showed no significant effect [new]. VisuAlgo+ therefore keeps VisuAlgo's curated examples alongside free editing, and the follow-up should test whether pseudocode, backward stepping and notes help.' This also gives LIT-2 the 'critical' element the rubric asks for.
- **Verifier:** raised by the verifier; not separately re-verified

#### `official-OFF-2` (Minor, finder said Important; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 16; Whole document; end matter after References; category official-length
- **Issue:** The official guideline describes the CA report as "a short report (10-15 pages)". It does not say whether the cover, references or notes count. The PDF has 16 pages and the last one is numbered 16, yet that page holds only two short notes (about 10 lines in footnote size). Without the cover the report is 15 pages and within range. With the cover it is 16, and the visible "16" invites that stricter count.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** pdftotext reports 16 pages (16 form feeds). en-16.png shows "Report scope and evidence" and "Source note", then blank space, footer 16. en-15.png is full of references. main.tex line 56 `\setcounter{page}{2}` counts the cover as page 1, and lines 369-371 place the notes after the bibliography with \vfill, so they spill onto a new page. OFFICIAL: CA Report Guidelines: "The CA Report is a short report (10-15 pages) that covers: ...", with no exclusions stated. The official Final Report Guidelines §2.2.9 (https://www.comp.nus.edu.sg/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/FYP-Report-Format-final_000.pdf) apply to the final report only, but give the School's convention: numbering "starts from the first page of Introduction", front pages use i, ii, and abstract, contents and references "are not counted".
- **Proposed fix:** Replace main.tex l.56 \setcounter{page}{2} with \pagenumbering{arabic}, placed just after \end{titlepage}. The cover stays unnumbered and the last page then reads 15. Optional: fold one scope sentence into §1.3 and move the 'VisuAlgo remains the source…' credit into §1.1. Delete the rest of page 16 so the PDF ends on page 15.
- **Verifier:** The facts check out: 16 form feeds; en-16.png holds only the two notes; main.tex l.56 is \setcounter{page}{2}; l.368-371 place the notes after the bibliography with \vfill. The guideline's '(10-15 pages)' is real and states no counting rule. However, the School's own convention (Final Report Guidelines §2.2.9, fetched) numbers pages from the Introduction, and references 'are not counted'. On that convention the body is 14 pages (pp.2-15), within range. Only a raw PDF-page count gives 16, so the risk is cosmetic, not a likely breach. I downgrade it from Important to Minor. Dropping the Source note wholesale is unwise: its VisuAlgo credit sentence matters (owner's Q7: credit stated wherever the material is used).

#### `crit-technical-TA-6` (Minor, finder said Important; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 6, 13; §4.3 Sorting uses a different execution model; category explanation-clarity
- **Issue:** An evaluator cannot follow the sorting execution model as written, and parts of it mislead. (1) 'Operation' is never defined. (2) 'primes streams up to a 60,000-operation aggregate threshold' omits why priming exists (so the scrubber's end is exact) and that the cap is summed across panes and spent round robin (so histories stay level). It also omits that every run at the default size is therefore produced whole, so 'This approach avoids requiring every sort to finish generating before interaction begins' is true only for large inputs. (3) 'Common operation index' is not defined: each pane shows min(tick, its length) operations. (4) 'Retained history remains unbounded' omits that input sizes are capped (160 generated values, 240 custom). The code's own note puts the worst run at about 159,000 operations.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** prototypes/src/sorting/comparison/operationBuffer.ts:7 (2,048). Same file :233-246: EAGER_OPERATION_CAP = 60,000, with a comment giving the default four panes at n=48 as 3,356 operations, n=160 as 25,015, the 240-value cap as 53,034, the worst UI choice as 159,298, and 'Retention is 105-180 bytes per operation, so this cap is 6-11 MiB'; this comment came from Bao's 5ccef45, 2026-09-25. Same file :262-275 (cap-and-bail 'so the scrubber's right-hand end is the real end') and :285-296 (round robin: two 40,000-operation panes once held 40,000 and 20,000). prototypes/src/sorting/app/useSortingExperiment.ts:21-25 (INITIAL_SIZE 48, MAX_GENERATED_SIZE 160, MAX_CUSTOM_VALUES 240). SortOperation is a union of 9 kinds (compare, swap, write, pivot, markSorted, auxWrite, bucket push/collect, phase). prototypes/docs/sorting/DESIGN_DECISIONS.md:159-202 (SP-05, SP-06). prototypes/src/sorting/comparison/seekComparison.test.ts:69, 83, 94. Report lines 316-327.
- **Proposed fix:** Keep §4.3 about its current length. - Define 'operation': a typed step such as compare, swap or write. - Say that priming makes the scrubber's end exact and is spent round robin, so pane histories stay level. - Say that runs within 60,000 operations, including every run at the default 48 values, are produced whole; only larger ones continue lazily. - Give the reason for replay: reset emptied the history the scrubber reads (SP-05). - Here and in §6.2, replace 'unbounded' with 'not capped by design; inputs are capped at 240 values, which the code records as at most about 159,000 operations'.
- **Verifier:** Reproduced. In operationBuffer.ts, 2,048 is the future-buffer capacity (line 7), so 'buffers up to 2,048' is accurate. The cap comment is at :232-246 (Bao, 5ccef45), cap-and-bail at :272-280 and round robin at :299-308; the finding's line numbers are off. useComparisonPlayer.ts:70-77 primes at mount, and useSortingExperiment.ts:21-25 caps input at 48/160/240. So every default run is produced whole before the first frame, and history is bounded in practice: about 159,298 operations at worst, per the code comment (DOCUMENTED). 'Cannot follow' is overstated; the paragraph is dense but accurate. The misleading parts are 'unbounded' (repeated as a 'material' limit on p.13) and a lazy benefit stated without its threshold.

#### `crit-technical-TA-11` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 11; §4.8, paragraph 'Timing adapts to playback'; category accuracy
- **Issue:** 'notes are suppressed for intervals below 200 milliseconds' misstates the rule. While a run plays, a step note lasts min(1,600 ms, 0.9 × step interval), and the note is dropped when that is under 200 ms. Notes therefore disappear for steps faster than about 222 ms. Step notes pass no delay, so 'configured delay' does not apply to them. The text also omits that notes appear only when a step is reached forward, not on rewind or seek, which is a deliberate design choice worth stating.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** prototypes/src/shared/visualization/floatingTextTiming.ts:9-13 (VALUE_POPUP_MS 800, FLOATING_NOTE_MS 1600, FLOATING_TEXT_MIN_MS 200) and :27-30 (cutToStep: min(life, 0.9 × interval − delay), undefined under 200). prototypes/src/shared/visualization/stepNotes.ts:43-48 (no note unless arrival.forward; delayMs: 0). Report lines 512-514.
- **Proposed fix:** Replace lines 512-514 as proposed, with the last sentence made precise: 'Notes appear only when a step is reached by Next or during playback; Previous, First, Last and the scrubber show none.'
- **Verifier:** Reproduced. - floatingTextTiming.ts:27-30: while playing, a note lasts min(1,600, 0.9 x interval - delay) and is dropped under 200 ms. - stepNotes.ts:43-48: no note unless arrival.forward; delayMs is 0. - useStepArrival.ts:5-9, 31: forward means exactly one step, by Play or Next. So notes vanish for intervals under about 222 ms. The delay applies only to segment-tree value popups (valuePopups.ts:91-92). Previous, First, Last and the scrubber show no note.

#### `crit-literature-LR-7` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 3 (and 5); §2.2 Reusable platforms and implementation choices; category accuracy-structure
- **Issue:** §2.2 does not match its title: it discusses no reusable AV platform (e.g. JSAV, the library under OpenDSA, or Algorithm Visualizer's tracers). Its first paragraph credits Shaffer et al. [5] with motivating 'maintainable implementations and reusable interaction patterns', which the paper does not argue, and then disclaims it ('does not establish ... or prove'). The second paragraph (React Flow, D3, Canvas 2D) describes products, compares no alternative, and repeats what §4.1 and Figure 1 say. The repository records give no reason for choosing React Flow (V1 already used it), so these implementation choices are stated, not justified.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** Shaffer et al. 2010 (ShafferTOCE11 PDF, pdftotext). Abstract: 'over 500 AVs ... many AVs are of low quality, and coverage is skewed toward a few easier topics ... no effective repositories ... at risk for being lost'. §5: 'most smaller developers of AVs do not seem aware of lessons learned from effectiveness studies ... repeating the same mistakes'. Editorial ratings: 18% Recommended, 42% Has Potential, 30% Not Recommended. Duplication: report-pages.txt lines 134-139 (p.3) vs lines 265-269 and Figure 1 (p.5). React Flow rationale: research-notes/02-standalone-repos.md:77 and :234 record the stack only; git grep finds no alternatives in prototypes/docs or design/proposals.
- **Proposed fix:** Replace ¶1 with Shaffer's findings and their consequence, in about 60 words as drafted. Before cutting ¶2, move its citations into §4.1 ¶2 ('React Flow [9] ... D3 [1]'). If objective 1 is to be argued, ask the owner why React Flow was kept from V1 and whether alternatives were weighed, and state that in one sentence. Otherwise, say it was carried over from the V1 prototype.
- **Verifier:** Partly reproduced. §2.2 ¶2 repeats §4.1 and Figure 1 (p.5). Shaffer's concrete findings go unused: over 500 AVs, many of low quality, coverage skewed to easy topics, AVs being lost, developers unaware of effectiveness results, and ratings of 18/42/30%. No record gives a reason for React Flow (git grep for alternatives: 0 hits). Two points are overstated: 'helps motivate' is a loose link, not a misattribution; and React Flow and D3 are reusable platforms, so the title point is a matter of taste. The fix contains an error: §4.1 does not cite [1] or [9]. They are cited only in §2.2 ¶2 (main.tex:103), so deleting ¶2 would drop both entries from the BibTeX list and renumber the references.

#### `crit-accuracy-ACC-18` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 11; 4.8; category technical-accuracy
- **Issue:** 'notes are suppressed for intervals below 200 milliseconds' is not the rule in the code. While a run plays, a note's life is cut to 90% of the step interval minus its delay, and the note is left out when less than 200 ms would remain. So even with no delay, notes disappear at intervals below about 222 ms.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** visualgo-plus-design/prototypes/src/shared/visualization/floatingTextTiming.ts:11-13 (FLOATING_NOTE_MS 1600, FLOATING_TEXT_MIN_MS 200), :22-30 (cutToStep: min(life, interval x 0.9 - delay); none below 200 ms).
- **Proposed fix:** 'Reached with Next, a note lasts 1,600 milliseconds. During autoplay it is cut to 90% of the step interval and left out when that would be under 200 milliseconds (intervals below about 220 ms). Stepping back or jumping shows no note.'
- **Verifier:** Reproduced, and the error is wider than stated. floatingTextTiming.ts:27-30 cuts a note during playback to min(1600, 0.9 × interval − delay) and drops it below 200 ms. stepNotes.ts:44-49 passes a delay of 0, so notes have no 'configured delay' and disappear at intervals below about 222 ms. Notes also appear only when a step is reached one forward by Play or Next (useStepArrival.ts:6-10), never on Previous, First, Last or the scrubber, so 'manually inspected' overstates. Doc 02:122 states the rule correctly.

#### `qa-QA-11` (Minor; CONFIRMED)

- **Where:** page 10 (also p.9); §4.7 Tree operations and sorting comparison (also §4.6, p.9); category validation
- **Issue:** The correctness story covers only the new graph algorithms. §4.7 never says how tree operations (AVL deletion, heap) are validated, although tests against independent references exist. On p.9, '700 graph inputs for cut-vertex/bridge checks' reads as 700 brute-force comparisons. In fact 400 are compared with brute force and 300 check the root rule, all on graphs of 1-10 vertices.
- **Evidence (VERIFIED):** App repo, all VERIFIED. createAvlDeleteSequence.test.ts:118-175 and :333-345: seeded random AVL trees of 1-40 keys have every key deleted, and each result is checked for BST order and balance, for the same shape as a separate textbook delete, and for a pseudocode line on every step. createHeapCreateSequence.test.ts:164-183: 200 random lists per order and method each build a valid heap of the same values. cutVertexBridgeProperty.test.ts:30, 86, 117-124: graphs have 1-10 vertices; seeds 1-400 are checked against brute force (more than 100 of them with a second edge to a DFS parent) and seeds 401-700 check the root rule. createBellmanFordSequence.test.ts:241-262: graphs have 1-6 vertices.
- **Proposed fix:** Add one sentence to §4.7: AVL Delete is compared with a textbook delete on seeded trees of up to 40 keys, deleting every key, and heap construction is checked on 200 random lists per order and method. On p.9, write '400 random graphs of up to 10 vertices against brute force (over 100 with a doubled edge to a DFS parent), plus 300 for the root rule'.
- **Verifier:** Reproduced. createAvlDeleteSequence.test.ts:333-345 deletes every key of eight seeded AVL trees of 1-40 keys and compares each result with a separate textbook delete; expectSoundDeletion (:117-175) also requires a pseudocode line on every step. createHeapCreateSequence.test.ts:164-183 builds 200 random lists per order and method. cutVertexBridgeProperty.test.ts uses graphs of 1-10 vertices (:30): seeds 1-400 are checked against brute force (:86), over 100 of them with a second edge to a DFS parent (:120), and seeds 401-700 check the root rule (:124). §4.7 says nothing about validating tree operations. '700 graph inputs' is literally true but reads as 700 brute-force checks.

One correction to the proposed note: a 'case' is what the test runner counts, and a parameterised test counts once per row. For example, the heap 'either Create' it.each (createHeapCreateSequence.test.ts:164-166) is 4 cases of 200 lists each, so 'one test() call' is not exact.

## Appendix C. Positives noted by the finders (generated; strengths to keep)

- [timeline] Standalone dates are correct: V1's first commit is f3a0db8 (28 Jul 2026). V2 starts with 92fa193 (20 Aug), with its state domains set in AGENTS.md fc62e5a (20 Aug), the playback session f864de7 (27 Aug) and ARCHITECTURE.md 0f5b49a (30 Aug), so 'V2 followed in August with a clearer separation of document, layout and playback state' holds. Sorting is 9236a65 (19 Aug); tree runs 3–17 Sep.
- [timeline] 'On 18 October, Kerk consolidated ... into one application and moved common code into a shared layer' is accurate. 0394b64 (Kerk Tai Heng, 2026-10-18 21:37 +08:00) came in through PR #20 (8d7a63a, merged by kerktaiheng) and is the first commit to create prototypes/src/shared ('The 40 files ... live once in src/shared'). Kerk's c39b3cf (PR #18), ae63a62 and c59c155/5235fcf (PR #21) landed the same evening.
- [timeline] 'During September and October, I adapted these visualizers to Kerk's design system' is supported by 0073875 (20 Sep: wireframe 2 V2/V7, top bar with the design system's nine themes), PR #4 (28 Sep: the design-system TopBar) and the V10 work through 18 Oct.

None of the ten builds deployed to Pages during Week 10 had floating text; it first went live with eb4c4d8 on 25 Oct. That matches the claim that floating text came after the trial.

The p.16 scope note matches Q1 and the 3/4 November clarifications, and the back matter says openly that the trial account supplements the pinned snapshot.

- [timeline] The evidence pack's open Q1 can now be closed: the official calendar confirms Week 1 began Mon 10 Aug 2026, as the owner said.

The five named as inherited are exactly graph-visualizer-v2 c8b14e6's five; recursive DFS was added later in 4ab075c. Tree has 7 workspaces (src/tree/app/workspaceModes.ts:3,14-22) and sorting has 8 ids (src/sorting/types/sort.ts:1-9). The DFS/Kahn and DFS/BFS methods match algorithmMethods.ts:37-51.

- [architecture] Table 2 matches the code. GraphDocument carries topologyRevision (src/graph/graph/model/graph.ts:24-29). GraphLayout holds positions plus a user-facing `fixed` pin flag (model/layout.ts:8-21; pin/unpin in app/PagePanel.tsx:130-131). The reducer ignores a tick unless the session is playing with the same sessionId (visualization/sessionReducer.ts:168). A weight edit bumps the revision and ends the run via topology-changed.
- [architecture] The numbers in 4.3 are exact: a 2,048-operation future buffer (operationBuffer.ts:7), priming capped at 60,000 operations summed across panes (:246, :292-327), and backward seeks that replay from a fresh state with no checkpoints (comparisonPlayback.ts:326-352). The sorting algorithms are generators; graph and tree have none (both are eager).
- [architecture] The V10 model claims hold. The canvas is not part of the model. parseLayout requires the four panels exactly once and allows extras at most once, repairing a bad save or falling back to the default (groupsModel.ts:688-802). Minimums are 240/120 (:86-90). One dropTransition serves preview, verdict and release (groupsModel.ts:1340-1356; GroupsLayout.tsx:553-554). Saves are versioned v:1.
- [architecture] The model-test description is accurate: 4,000 seeded steps (mulberry32 seed 20261017), five page sizes, three drawing floors, and a serialize/parse round trip every 25 steps (groupsModel.test.ts:1210-1340).
- [architecture] The default arrangement in the text, Figure 2 and Figure 3 matches DEFAULT_LAYOUT (groupsModel.ts:108-120): one left group with Action and e-Lecture, two right groups (Pseudocode, Status), and an empty bottom row. Figure 4 shows exactly the preview that previewFor draws for joining a group: a framed body plus a caret.
- [architecture] The tree preview/Apply claim is real. 'Apply insertion' ('Node X is still temporary') is offered only on the final step (BstInsertInspector.tsx:58-64; getCommittableTreeViewProjection, algorithmIntegration.ts:466-473). The same Apply path exists for delete, AVL, heap, trie and segment operations.
- [architecture] 'No runtime connection' to wasm-arch is verified: nothing in prototypes/src imports it or uses WebAssembly, workers or the network. wasm-arch is indeed a WebAssembly + worker/RPC project (wasm-arch/README.md, web/src/engine/worker.ts).
- [architecture] The library claims are verified in prototypes/package.json and in the imports: React 19, TypeScript, Vite 8, @xyflow/react, d3-force (graph), d3-hierarchy (tree, including heap, trie and segment views), and d3-interpolate/d3-ease with Canvas 2D (sorting).
- [architecture] The limitation statements on browser coverage are honest. No e2e spec drags or splits a tab: prototypes/e2e only moves tabs through the tab menu and drags a column splitter (graph.spec.ts:299-390).
- [algorithms] The 11 graph algorithm ids and the Method options (topological sort: DFS/Kahn; bipartite: DFS/BFS) match the registry exactly: prototypes/src/graph/algorithms/algorithmIntegration.ts:137-210 and algorithmMethods.ts:36-51.
- [algorithms] The parallel-edge paragraph matches the code. The parent edge is skipped by edge id (createCutVertexBridgeSequence.ts:169-175), and any other edge to the parent vertex is treated as a back edge (:197-223). The unit test createCutVertexBridgeSequence.test.ts:118-138 and the e2e test graph-traversal.spec.ts:402-421 (CP4 4.22) both cover it.
- [algorithms] The oracle descriptions are accurate. Cut Vertex & Bridge is checked by removing a vertex or edge and recounting components (cutVertexBridgeProperty.test.ts:45-73). Bellman-Ford is checked against a Floyd-Warshall all-pairs oracle that includes negative-cycle reachability (createBellmanFordSequence.test.ts:223-262). Prim is checked by exhaustive edge-subset search (primEngine.test.ts:51-77). The 140 and 20-from-every-start counts are right.
- [algorithms] The Figure 5 values are correct. I recomputed them by hand from CP3_4_9 (visualgoExamples.ts:90-98); they match the PNG, including the num,low badges, and graph-traversal.spec.ts:354-376 asserts them.
- [algorithms] The 'witnesses' claim holds. The DFS topological sort stops on a cycle (createTopologicalSortDfsSteps.ts:131-140). Kahn ends by listing the stuck vertices, each with indegree above zero, which is a valid certificate of a cycle (createKahnSteps.ts:125-142). Both bipartite methods stop on an odd cycle (createBipartiteCheckDfsSteps.ts:132-140, createBipartiteCheckBfsSteps.ts:120-128).
- [algorithms] The captions of Figures 3, 4 and 7 match their PNGs: Frame 8/22 in the default layout; the drag preview with the framed Pseudocode group, ghost label and dashed bottom band; Merge/Bubble/Quick/Radix at size 48.
- [algorithms] The tree and sorting claims are verified. There are seven workspaces (src/tree/app/workspaceModes.ts:14-22). The catalogs hold the BST queries, traversals, AVL Delete and nine heap operations. The lazy segment tree's Range Update is a range add (createLazyRangeUpdateSequence.ts:19-29), and the heap has a preview with Apply (HeapMode.tsx:49). Sorting has 1/2/4 panes for Study and Compare (sortingCatalog.ts:5-9). Duplicate panes are disabled (useSortingExperiment.ts:373-381). The randomized quicksort pivot is seeded from the dataset seed (comparisonSorts.ts:277-285, useSortingExperiment.ts:262).
- [algorithms] The hedging in sections 4.6-4.7 is appropriate and should stay: 'not a formal proof', 'a playback coordinate, not a measurement of relative runtime', 'do not imply complete operation parity'.
- [floating] The coverage sentence is exactly right. In the graph, only Dijkstra writes notes: createAlgorithmStepNotes returns NO_STEP_NOTES for the other ten kinds (algorithmIntegration.ts:516-540). In the tree, notes cover LCA (both runs), every BST operation (search, min, max, predecessor, successor, lower bound, select, the three traversals, insert, delete), AVL inspection, insert and delete, and the Binary Heap (TreeMode.tsx:199-217, 560; HeapMode.tsx:305). Trie, Segment Tree, Lazy Segment Tree and sorting have none.
- [floating] The architecture sentence is accurate. Shared code covers the note description and writer (stepNotes.ts), placement (stepNotePlacement.ts), timing (floatingTextTiming.ts, useStepArrival.ts) and rendering (StepNoteLayer.tsx, FloatingText.tsx). The algorithms supply only the text and the tone.
- [floating] The placement summary is accurate: above the node first, then right, left or below, with a least-covered fallback; badges, PINs and weight labels count as obstacles (stepNotePlacement.ts:177-223; stepNoteAnchor.ts:188-211). The overlap caveat is honest. DD-61's measurement could back it: 112 of 444 shipped notes touch a mark as they pop in on Big, Large and MRT at 1440×900 (docs/graph/DESIGN_DECISIONS.md:2752).
- [floating] The 1,600 ms life after Next (FLOATING_NOTE_MS, floatingTextTiming.ts:11; test lines 12-18) and the aria-hidden layer are both verified.
- [floating] Figure 8's caption matches the image and DD-61 frame 24: a green '4 + 3 < 8 · update' over E, the 8→7 badge, and the same verdict in the Status sentence.
- [floating] The hedges are appropriate and should stay: 'a design aim, not a measured result', 'The effect of floating text has not been measured', and 'Step notes cover only selected algorithms and may overlap'.

- [crit-literature] The bibliographic details of [2], [3] and [5] are correct: venue, volume and pages, plus Shaffer's full author list including 'Alexander Joel D. Alon'. All external URLs except [4] resolve.

- [crit-literature] The p.16 source note honestly labels [6]-[8] as working evidence rather than research publications. Keep it, but move it up.

- [crit-management] Scope limits are stated honestly (engine not integrated, no learning or comparative claims, trial limits in 6.2), so the plan does not over-promise.
- [crit-management] Section 7 puts strengthening a coherent subset ahead of catalogue growth and names scope growth as the main project risk. That is sound judgement for the final semester.

- [crit-management] Table 5 already pairs every window with completion evidence; only owners, dependencies and measurable values are missing.
- [crit-management] Table 4 and 6.4 define concrete tasks and a repeatable measurement method (fixed machine, at least five repeats, median and spread).
- [crit-management] Section 3.2 separates Bao's and Kerk's responsibilities in line with the owner's dated answers, and the AI paragraph is enough as it stands.
- [crit-report] The structure maps onto the official CA items: objectives (§1.3), related work (§2), progress (§3-5), plan (§6.3-7) and references.
- [crit-report] The abstract is a real abstract: 181 words, one limitation sentence out of ten, with concrete counts. Putting it on the cover saves a page.
- [crit-report] The TikZ diagrams (Figures 1-2) are vector, legible at caption size, and their captions explain the dashed elements. Keep them as they are.
- [crit-report] Figure 8's caption quotes the on-screen note (4 + 3 < 8, so E goes from 8 to 7). It is the model for the other captions.
- [crit-report] Table 2's 'Example consequence' column and Table 5's 'Completion evidence' column make design and plan items concrete and checkable.
